ParlaMint-DK_20210413130027	The meeting is open.
ParlaMint-DK_20210413130033	I welcome the extended party leader debate. Two years ago, we started an experiment with party leader debate in this Chamber, and this is the fourth time we have held it, but this is the first time we have the North Atlantic mandates. The aim of the trial scheme is to allow party leaders to debate here in the Parliamentary Assembly without a specific subject being set out in advance and without government participation in its capacity as government. Each party leader has three minutes' speaking time from here, the speakers are held in general order of parties, and for each speech the other party leaders have two brief comments of 1⁄2 minutes and at the same time the time for replying. It is the case that today's series of speeches will be after the general order of the parties, but I have chosen to respond to a request from Mrs Aaja Chemnitz Larsen, IA, to speak earlier to enable Mrs Aaja Chemnitz Larsen, on behalf of the Folketing, to take part in a debate on the Kingdom Community in the parliamentary congress around the Arctic. I therefore give the floor to Mrs Aaja Chemnitz Larsen, IA. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20210413130156	Thank you very much to the President-in-Office for that. In the Kingdom Community we have an inherent dilemma: Denmark needs to let go in order to preserve the Kingdom community. Together, therefore, we should also have an interest in strengthening the level of education and vocational development in Greenland. These are two crucial factors to ensure Greenland's development • a development that should not be feared by the Danish side but, on the contrary, supported. Many who have followed the election campaign may have noticed that the Danish media have reported that the Greenlandic election was primarily about independence. It's not fair. The election was also very much about Kuannersuit, the Kvanefjeldet, it was about welfare, and it was about the close. It was about whether we can provide help for vulnerable children and people with disabilities, about how we create better and more education for our young people, and about how we improve public health in Greenland and provide a dignified old age for the elderly. At electoral meetings around the country in Greenland, the population asked about the welfare challenges they are experiencing: increasing inequality, how to help prevent sexual abuse, violence and suicide, and how to get more people into work. That is the welfare that many people asked about at the electoral meetings and which the elections were also about. As Inuit Ataqatigiit has repeated here in the Folketing and also at home in Greenland, we are unfortunately in a welfare crisis, and our priority is to get that task done. That is what the electorate voted for, not promises of independence tomorrow. The coming government in Greenland is part of the new generation of politicians, of which I myself am also a part. We are children of home rule, we are children of self-government, and we have never known anything but the desire to be master of our own house. In other words, we are also products of our time, and I hope that we will see a more solution-oriented, but also a more thorough and clearer approach to the way in which our country should go and the way in which it should go, both individually and jointly. We may not hear it very often, but the Commonwealth is really a model which is a role model for many other countries and which indigenous people look to as the good example. ParlaMint-DK_20210413160722	Thank you. I will conclude today's debate with a small matter which is even so unknown that none of those sitting in the Chamber know about it at all. Many would also say that it is too small to address such a fine forum, but I do not think so. Before I took the platform, I therefore put some pictures on my Facebook and Instagram, so you can see what I am talking about, namely a Danish wall that stands in the middle of Thorshavn. Within the wall, the Richman hangs out. It says what we call the county castle. When it was built on a high hill in 1881, it was a castle compared to the city's other buildings. A few years later, a wall was built between the large garden around the castle and the main street of the city. It is not a Chinese wall or Berlin wall or Palestine wall or Trumpmur or pay wall, but like all these walls it is a political wall. It's up to 31⁄2 meters tall. Therefore, the Privateman is not merely denied access to the Kingdom Ombudsman's oasis at the city's finest address; he cannot even look into it. The symbolism is thick and almost primitive in its clarity, and whether it is on purpose or not, it communicates the arrogance of power. The Danish officials were in the castle inside the wall; the local citizens were outside. The wall symbolized a power that ruled over the Faroese people, but at the same time was separated from the people. And this has not always been the case throughout the ages; many of the countymen were very committed to Faroese society. But the wall expressed something else, and it still does away with it. When tourists ask why a central stretch of Thorshavn's main street is dark, entangled, dull, sad and dead, one has to answer that behind the dark wall it was that Denmark's representative in the Faroe Islands became entrenched. The town council in Thorshavn now wants the wall taken down, and the reason I'm taking it up today is that it's the PM's department. You shouldn't sweep it off the table and say it's a matter for the Building Agency or anyone else. The wall is a political matter. We should therefore welcome the political desire to remove it in whole or in part. That would be a good and a wise and a popular political decision with a clear symbolism and a clear message. If you say no, it will also be a political decision with a clear symbolism and a clear message. And no one can want to send that message in 2021, because it does not reflect reality at all. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210413164020	Thank you for that, President. I have asked for the floor for this debate as Vice-President of the Committee on the Rules of Procedure, and I have done so because I need just to clarify an inappropriate wording in the comments on the new rules which we are now introducing into the Rules of Procedure of the Danish Parliament, and I do so in order that we can, as it were, clarify, even for posterity, as to what is to apply. The administration of the Folketing has become aware that a wording has been included in the comments to the new section 65. 1, which is not entirely adequate for what was intended. It is a question of how the Committee on the Rules of Procedure, the Verification of Credentials and Immunities, on the basis of a recommendation from the Committee on the Verification of Credentials, the Verification of Credentials and Immunities, should take steps to set up a committee of inquiry. The comments state that the Committee on the Rules of Procedure will not be able to decide independently that the committee of inquiry or a commission of inquiry should be set up, but that the Committee on the Rules of Procedure would have to draw up and submit motions for resolutions on the setting-up. As far as investigative committees are concerned, it just does not quite agree with what was intended. The intention, as it is also described in the report of the Committee on the Rules of Procedure of December 2020, which has formed the basis for the proposed new rules, has been that the Committee on the Rules of Procedure, the Verification of Credentials and Immunities, itself on the basis of a recommendation from the Committee on the Verification of Credentials and Immunities, should be able to decide that a committee of inquiry should be set up, and it is only in the case that there is a minority of at least one third who are specifically against the committee of inquiry or simply would like the matter to be dealt with in the Chamber that it would be appropriate to draw up and table a motion for a resolution so that the matter could be dealt with in this Chamber. The possibility for a committee to decide on behalf of the Danish Parliament that a committee of inquiry should be set up is also provided for in the comments to the new rules of the law on committees of inquiry and investigations, which we have just dealt with thirdly. As far as committees of inquiry are concerned, it is true that what is stated in the comments is due to the fact that in future it will also be the case that it will require a parliamentary decision to instruct the Minister of Justice to set up a commission of inquiry. So, with this speech, I hope that the matter has been clarified so that it is clear in posterity how the new Rules of Procedure on this point are to be understood. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210413170531	After all, this is a legislative proposal, which is the execution of an agreement that has been concluded in the past. I think it is important to emphasise that, in general terms, it can be said that our social model is based on a principle that everyone who can and has the skills should contribute and that our services are targeted at those who have the greatest need, where we take care of those who cannot support themselves. It must be said that we are in a very extraordinary situation where it requires something extra, and there we need everyone who can take an extra boost, and fortunately a lot of people have done so, and very many thanks for that. However, it can be said that, on the basis of our general principle of right and duty, several have experienced a financial penalty in the form of a reduction in the pension of their own or their spouse or their spouse as a consequence of a higher income in connection with additional work in relation to the Coronapandemic. This is also why, on 24 February of this year, the government entered into an agreement on no set-off in pension for extra income in relation to COVID-19... and it was in addition to the Social Democracy together with the Left, the Danish People's Party, the SF, the Radical Left, the Enhedslisten and New Citizens. With this agreement, we are ensuring that citizens who make or have made an extra effort during the Coronapandemic do not meet with an economic sanction, and thus we also recognise their great contribution in relation to the Coronapandemic. I know that in previous discussions there has been such a little question of saying: but there was such a concept called corona heroes, which was an easy and accessible way to launch it here in the press. I am not going to go into that further, but more in the discussion of the question: is this something recurring? It has to be said that, in the situation we have faced, we have needed all the good powers, and we do not think it should be the case that the citizens refrain from contributing because they meet with economic sanctions. So, as I have said, the draft law we will be considering must be a proposal aimed at implementing the legislative elements contained in that agreement of 24 February, and that is why we support the draft law in the Social Democratic Party.

ParlaMint-DK_20210413182610	With the motion for a resolution, the authors of the motion for a resolution want to require the government not to introduce a right to a legal change of sex for children, a right which the majority of Parliament has expressed its wish to introduce. I would like to start by explaining the government's proposal on legal gender change for children and young people, and why the government thinks it is a good idea. As the authors are aware, legal gender change is not possible under current law for persons under 18 years of age. In the Government's initiative of August 2020, the government has expressed a desire to introduce a right to legal gender change for children and young people following a declaration model. The declaration model will give persons under 18 years of age the right to legal change on the basis of a declaration that the child or young person experiences belonging to the other sex. The Government proposes that consent from parents, the holders of authority is required for children under 15 years of age. It does not propose a lower age limit for the right to legal change of sex, but proposes that, prior to the change of legal sex, the child or young person should receive legal advice equivalent to that of age, so that the child or young person should be involved and the decision taken on an informed basis. But let me first explain why it is the political desire of the government to go this way. This is because many children and young people are caught up because this opportunity is not available today. This applies, among other things, to: the children and young people who, since 2016, where possible, have sought health care through gender identity and have been referred to the investigation and hormone treatment in health care, or the children and young people who have changed their first name to a gender-neutral or not gender-correct name and have a gender expression and physical appearance that does not match their biological sex. Such children and young people experience difficulties when their physical appearance is not consistent with their social security number. Therefore, the government's view is that we should meet these children and young people where they are, precisely in the experience of their own gender identity. As a society, we can easily and simply help children and young people in this situation, by changing their social security number to a social security number that denotes the other sex, without being intrusive either to the individual or to others in society, because the personal number can be changed very simply and easily back again. A joint statement by LGBT+ Denmark on behalf of a number of organisations in this area has indicated that they want a change in the legal gender of minors to be possible without a lower age limit. The Children's Council, Children's Conditions and Save the Child have also indicated that they are positive about legal gender changes for children and young people. Most recently, a significant majority in the Ethics Council has recommended that a change of legal gender should be possible for children under the age of 18, just as the majority also recommend that this be done through a model of declaration as proposed in the government proposal. I understand that it is tempting to consider an age limit. The government's initiative is to leave it to the child or young person himself, together with his parents, to decide when a legal change of sex is right, because it is very much about the child and the young person's perception of gender identity. At the same time, it is an administrative procedure which can be reversed at any time. In addition, the inter-ministerial working group, which has carried out the legislative review which is the basis for the government's lgbti initiative, has found that it is difficult within the framework of Denmark's international obligations to set a lower age limit for legal gender change. This is due, among other things, to the fact that there is no absolute lower age limit for when a child can begin a sex-modifying treatment in the health care system at the earliest, nor is there any evidence of establishing such an absolute lower age limit. The government has also taken serious note of the recommendation of a majority in the Ethics Council for an age limit of 10 to 12 years for legal gender change, and this will be part of the government's further work. In the comments to B 80, the proposers point out that a child is not necessarily aware of what a legal gender change entails, just as the child is in hormonal development and is therefore not necessarily consistent in its desire to change sex. It also refers to the fact that regard for the best interests of the child and its right to protection argue against the introduction of a right to a legal change of sex, regardless of parental consent. Finally, it is pointed out that legal gender change for children is a major decision on law and ethics. The government believes that children and young people have the right to live a life in which they gain legal recognition for the gender with which they identify. It is about offering a new social security number to a very small group of children and young people who are miserable because of a lack of consistency between their experienced gender identity and the biological sex with which they were born, and this is precisely an offer to which we are at the same time linking an age-matching legal advice to ensure that the decision is taken on an informed basis. Should it prove to be the wrong way to go for the individual, the decision can be quickly changed by redistributing the original social security number without any irreparable damage either to the individual or to society. The Government therefore expects to include a legislative proposal on the legal gender change for children and young people in the legislative programme for 2021-22. For the reasons I have mentioned, therefore, the government cannot support the resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20210413184643	Thank you for the floor. In the social democracy we believe that all people should have the opportunity to be who they are and to live according to their gender identity. This is crucial for the well-being and quality of life of the individual. In our proposal from August 2020 •Freedom to diversity • Enhanced rights and opportunities for LGBTI people • We expressed our desire to enable people under 18 to change legal gender to match the gender with which each individual identifies. With a change in legislation, we want to signal that we are giving room for diversity in Denmark. We stress that tranners have just as much right to be part of society and to be themselves as all other citizens have. The change of legal gender must be a common and uncontroversial decision placed in the hands of individuals and families. Legislation can help change culture and promote acceptance in society. Since 2016, it has been possible for children and young people with a desire for health care assistance in terms of gender identity to be referred to investigation and hormone treatment in health care. Children and young people today also have the opportunity to change their first name so that it is consistent with their gender identity. However, according to current law, legal change of sex is not possible for persons under the age of 18 as it is today. Therefore, some tranquilized children and young people are faced with the situation that their gender expressions, names and physical appearances do not match with their legal sex. For these children and young people, a common situation such as a visit to the library or the dentist may lead to unnecessary anxiety and nervousness or distraction. This may also mean that children and young people who have been assessed are excluded in social contexts where participation is gender-separated on the basis of the number of persons. We can avoid this by giving them the possibility of legal gender change. That is what the children themselves, their families and the civil society organisations that represent them are asking for. Of course, we should listen to that and take it seriously. I think we owe it to them. A large majority of the Ethics Council supports the initiative and recommends a reduction in the age limit for legal gender change following a declaration model. According to the Ethics Council, children and young people's lack of legal gender change means that problems of explanation, suspicion, exclusion or harassment arise for the individual in the meeting with the outside world. At the same time, the Ethical Council stresses that it is possible to help this small group of minors without having a high cost to the rest of society. I understand, then, that the government still expects the legislative proposal on the legal gender change for children and young people to be adopted in the legislative programme 2021-2022, and here, through a thorough legislative process with a broad and • that I expect, at least external consultation, there will be an opportunity to highlight some of the concerns raised by the proposers today. The opinions of the Ethical Council will also be included in the further deliberations on the draft law on the change of legal gender for minors. In the social democracy we are in favour of being able to be who you are, whether you are a child, a young person or an adult. Social democracy cannot therefore support the motion for a resolution. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210413190604	Thank you very much. We in the Left believe that we must stick to common sense in the debate on children and gender identity and use our strength to help those children who need it. Like the authors of the motion for a resolution, we believe that care should be taken to introduce a general right to legal gender change for children, as it can be a rather large and far-reaching decision for a child to take. At the same time, children's gender identity is often not fully developed and can change on the way to adulthood. We all hear and read the stories around people who say that yes, they also wanted to be the opposite sex until they were 10 years old, 12 years old, 15 years old. However, we must also recognise that there are some children suffering from gender dysphoria, which is a recognised medical condition in which a person's gender identity does not correspond to the sex that has been awarded to him at birth. These children and young people are having a very difficult time. It can cause some unnecessary scars on the soul when they experience that the gender they feel is not accepted. We know that the figures for suicidal thoughts, self-harm, eating disorders among these children are very high and much higher than those of children who do not have the concerns of those children who fortunately feel at home in their body and gender. To be quite honest, I do not think they are thinking at all, and how wonderful it is. But how lovely it is for their parents, their siblings, their relatives, because we all know that if someone does, you listen to the next of kin, you will soon realize that they are always on guard, because they must always try to prevent their child from being again subjected to humiliation, which is based on the fact that their legal sex does not match their appearance. These are curious questions, puzzling looks, disbelief, so the feeling of being wrong. The only thing these kids really want is to be normal and not stick out, but settle in. Parents are in no doubt, and honestly, who knows their children best? The parents, I think, far, far along the way. Specialists recommend that these children and young people meet with recognition and understanding from the surroundings, and therefore it may be a good idea to be able to change the last four digits of the CPR number so that they feel accepted in everyday situations, as when they go to the dentist or have to go through the passport control. There are many examples that actions that we others don't give the slightest thought can actually provoke anxiety among them. It is a decision that affects and has an impact on those who have to make the choice, but has no harmful effect on others. That is to say, it is a change that can be changed again. In the Left, therefore, we want to help and accommodate those who really want to change their genders, but at the same time we also want to protect the children in general so that they do not make hasty decisions, and that is why we take the view that children and young people between 15 and 17 can get legal change if their parents consent to it. The 15-year limit is a choice, as it is also the limit for consent in health care, where you yourself have quite a high degree of co-determination at the age of 15. It is important that we think carefully about the issue of legal gender change for children under the age of 15. It may be easy for some to fall into automatic reactions to reduce the proposal from just a right to change the last four digits to be another crazy idea, but there I just have to say that this is serious and that we owe it to those people to take this subject seriously and to treat it with respect and understanding for the group which is very small but which it also means very, very much too. I repeat that at the same time we are and should be aware, as I said, that we politicians must not impose too much responsibility on the child. In the Left, we are very concerned that the legislation is being adapted so that it helps the very right children and young people who need it, and we cannot therefore support the proposal.

I think that legal gender change should be based on a thorough and individual assessment, because children are evolving differently. Therefore, it should also be up to professionals who really know something about this topic to assess when it is for the child's best to change the CPR number, not up to the Folketing. We cannot take individual consideration from this Chamber, and that is what is really needed among these children. The authors of this motion for a resolution justify the fact that children should be allowed to be children, that they should not be pressured to take a position on their gender identity, and I therefore fully agree with that. But we are forcing them to do that every day today, when we do not allow them to change sex, because we are pretty, as soon as we breathe for the first time. It just doesn't matter to the vast majority of children because they are born with the gender they identify with. However, when we limit the few beautiful children to being allowed to change legal genders, we do not let them be who they are, and thus we do not allow them to be children either. As I have said, the SF cannot support this proposal; on the contrary, I welcome the fact that it seems that there is a majority in favour of lowering the age limit, so that we can also make children who are beautiful.

There's a big resounding "no" from the kiosk man when the boy asks for this scratch card. He's just too small for that. The boy then presents a note from his mother and father that says he can buy a scratch card, but the answer is still a very clear no. He is told that he must be 16 years old to buy a scratch card and a lottery ticket, and that if one day he wants to odds a football match, he must wait until he is 18 years old, because it is a so-called knowledge game. The same boy now gets really hurt in the head, because he is tired of not buying a scratch card, even though his parents have given him permission and money to do so, so he wants to buy a pack of aspirin. He is immediately told that he certainly can't, because you have to be 18 years old to buy it. The boy then goes to his room and sits in a tiny bit of a slur. He has often thought that he doesn't feel quite right. He has had a great deal of thought and he has come to the conclusion that he wants to change legal gender. He goes out and talks to his parents about it, and they agree to it, so at the age of 14 he can change legal gender. Had the parents said no, he could have waited two months until he was 15 years old, and then, unhindered by what his parents thought, he could have changed legal gender. One can then say on reflection that it is good that our boy here did not sit in his room and remembered that he was going to buy a New Year's rocket, for that is what you have to be 18 years old for. Frankly, I think it is rather baroque that, in what is at least a majority in the Danish Parliament, it is thought that one can easily understand the consequences of changing sex legally at the age of 15 without the consent of the parents, but that the same majority in the Danish Parliament does not seem to think that one should buy an aspirin or odds at a football match before one becomes 18 years old. I do not need to say that I fully support the resolution tabled by most blue parties.

ParlaMint-DK_20210413201919	Thank you for the floor. The Danish People's Party has tabled this motion for a resolution which obliges the government to ensure, before the end of the parliamentary year, that in future the endorsement of mayors and presidents of the regional councils will be offset to a greater extent than is the case today. The authors of the motion for a resolution have pointed out that the resignation of mayors and presidents of the regional councils today will only lapse if the politician joins as a member of parliament, minister, mayor or chairman of the regional council. On the other hand, there shall be no set-off in the remuneration of a resigning mayor or chairman of the Regional Council if he receives other income from non-political office. This is a subject which the Folketing has also dealt with in the past. It was last discussed by the Social and Home Affairs Committee, which submitted a report on the issue on 11 February 2021. In the report, a majority of the Committee of Social Democrats, the Left and the Conservative People's Party expresses the view that the question of the post-recommendation of mayors and presidents of the regional councils raises an important discussion. However, the majority also state that the rules on reimbursement cannot be seen in isolation from the overall remuneration and pension conditions of full-time politicians. The parties therefore propose that a meeting of the Ministry of Home Affairs and Home Affairs be convened so that the rapporteurs can discuss the rules on set-off and follow-up for mayors and presidents of the regional councils. To begin with, I would like to say that I agree with the majority of the report text. I agree that there is something we need to look at here, but I also agree that we cannot simply decide to copy the rules for parliamentary politicians. The government will therefore not vote in favour of the motion for a resolution. On the other hand, we will convene a meeting of the parties so that we can discuss the various views and options in greater detail. If we turn to the content of the proposal, I agree that it may seem illogical that a mayor and a chairman of the Regional Council can keep their copy if the person concerned goes directly into a well-paid director's job. This is particularly true if you look at the post-mortem layer in isolation as an economic safety net. But I do not think it is quite as simple to change the terms as the proposers will do. The aftermath is just one element among the overall conditions for politicians in terms of remuneration, remuneration and pensions, and I therefore believe that we must look at whether the whole is still coherent if one element is to be adjusted. Then it is true that there is a difference between the set-off rules for mayors and regional councillors in relation to the set-off rules for members of parliament and ministers. But there is also a difference between how the post-regulation rules have, moreover, been tightened up. For example: You earn the right to vote as a member of parliament and minister more quickly than you do as mayor and chairman of the regional council. And members of parliament and ministers of ministers can be re-elected for a long time. If we are to standardize one place, then we can also ask whether other areas need to be standardized. In addition, I would like to mention that the issue of offsetting was also dealt with by the Salaries Commission. Here, the majority of the Commission did not recommend the set-off model that is being proposed here. That was among other things. on the grounds that the rules on the offsetting of members of parliament and ministers had proved extremely complicated to understand and difficult to administer in practice. The rules are based on the fact that politicians themselves report relevant income, which is to be reduced by the post-regulation. In this context, the Commission noted that transparency and transparency should also apply to politicians themselves and that, therefore, politicians should not be subject to rules on disclosure which are so difficult to understand that guidance is often necessary from, inter alia, the Member States. the managing authorities. That is why, in my view, the set-off rules for members of parliament and ministers are not so straightforward to copy. The government cannot therefore support the motion for a resolution. However, we are obviously open to discussing the various points of view in greater detail and will, as I have already said, convene the parties to a meeting on this matter.

ParlaMint-DK_20210413205419	The debate is open. Sir. Kasper Sand Kjær, Social Democracy.
ParlaMint-DK_20210413205427	Thank you for the floor. Today, or rather this evening, we are discussing an important legislative proposal which contains many parts and details and perhaps also a number of techniques, but which, in its essence, is about two things. It is about updating copyright for digital development and about strengthening rights of rightholders against tech giants and the new online platforms. The aim of the draft law is to implement two EU directives, on the one hand, the implementation of parts of the directive on copyright and related rights in the digital internal market and, on the other hand, the full implementation of the directive on copyright and related rights. The draft law proposes, among other things: to change the copyright regulation of what we call information society services, so that publishers of press publications, if their content is used online, are strengthened with new rights. This allows the media to negotiate collective agreements with the tech giants for payment for the use of their press publications. In addition, the draft law strengthens the rights holders in relation to online content sharing services so that the sharing services become responsible for it, which their users upload on the platform. In addition, it is also part of the proposal to introduce rules that will allow distributors to conclude agreements on the further distribution of programmes on the Internet by introducing a proper payment requirement. In addition to these amendments, the draft law also includes two extensions which are not directly related to the two EU directives. This is partly an extension which means that independent streaming services which transmit content are also covered by the law, and partly an extension which means that the rights of remuneration of sound and image producers in relation to cable transmission are also included. So there are a number of elements in the draft law, and it is all changes to existing legislation that will strengthen rights holders. It is both in relation to those who produce TV, those who produce sound, those who make music, and those who write press publications. It will strengthen their rights to the tech giants. This is essential to ensure that Danish artists, producers and media are paid for their work, and at the same time it is necessary to amend the law to ensure that the tech giants do not have competitive advantages due to new technology that the existing legislation does not take into account. The Tech giants basically provide a service that none of us will be left out of, but that does not mean that we should just let them profit from an ecosystem that they are undermining. Every day, Danish rightholders produce content that tech giants from abroad earn a lot of. The balance of negotiations between the rights holders and the tech giants is quite unbalanced today. That is why the draft law intends to restore that balance and create a much fairer marketplace where right-holders are stronger in their negotiations with the tech giants. It is the only way to support some more reasonable market conditions and ensure a fair payment to rightholders. That is why the Social Democratic Party supports the bill. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210413210228	Thank you. There are no more brief comments, and that is why we thank the rapporteur. And then it's sir. Kim Valentin, Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20210413210259	Thank you, Mr President. Yes, this bill is indeed a technical one. The purpose of this draft law is in fact to implement Articles 15 and 17 of the directive on copyright and related rights in the digital internal market, also known as the DSM Directive, and the directive on the transmission of radio and television programmes, also known as the SatCabII Directive. That explanation alone is technical enough in itself, but there is reason in it all, you will find out. The most attentive part of the legislative proposal is then the implementation part of the two provisions of the DSM Directive. It is also in this, it can be said, that the devil is in the detail. According to the draft law, services such as: Facebook and Google have the consent of a press publisher to use the publisher's material online. However, there are exceptions where no consent is required. The exceptions are as follows: private or non-commercial use of individual users; hyperlinks; single words and very short extracts from press publications. These are three important exceptions in this context. Of course, it is also important to know exactly what it means, and perhaps that is where the devil lies in the detail. This is one of the things we need to examine in the context of a technical consultation and in the context of asking more questions about understanding these things along the way. I think we should be 100% sure that we understand this in a similar way at least what's here. In fact, these were some of the questions that were asked before. We need to be absolutely sure what we're saying yes to with this proposal if we say yes. In addition, a specific contract licence is introduced for the use of press publications by these services so that the rights can be clarified collectively by a rights organisation and the copyright licensing board will have the power to decide in the event of disagreement. It is the kind of negotiating table that you set up, and it is also one of the things that is wanted from out there, so I suppose there is nothing wrong with that other than that there is also a detail here: what exactly is the role of such a negotiating table? It is not because we are in any way negative; it just needs to be clarified what lies in it, even if the comments on the legislative proposal are fairly precise. The bill will also make online content sharing services responsible for the content that their users upload to the platform. This means that services such as YouTube, where users upload content, are responsible for respecting copyright to the content. There are also some questions in this regard. The Left is therefore in favour of the proposal. We want to help strengthen Danish-produced content by strengthening the Danish media houses. The Left is also, as a starting point, pleased that agreements can be clarified collectively and that it will be voluntary for the press publishers whether they want to be outside these opt-outs. As you know, the Left welcomes free elections. However, we will be asking further questions about this in committee and during the technical consultation, as this is not a requirement under the EU directive. Finally, I would like to say that the draft law also contains amendments based on the SatCabII directive. The changes mean new rules for resubmitting and re-utilising content that is copyright protected. It is proposed, among other things, that: established rules on independent streaming services to include them in the retransmission system on an equal footing with radio and television organisations and to extend the direct injection principle of the SatCabII Directive, which means that all services must engage in distribution activities in Denmark. The Left is therefore in favour of the proposal and looks forward to the technical consultation and there will be questions on the above points in the legislative procedure. Thank you.
